Intelligence Assessment

Israel: Hung Knesset Likely; Deadlock and a 2027 Rerun the Single Most Likely Outcome

Netanyahu is very unlikely to form a new government (19%) but likely, if only marginally, to still be prime minister on 1 June 2027 (56%), mostly as caretaker during a deadlock; Gadi Eisenkot is the likeliest head of any government formed in this Knesset.

Prepared forChief Political Analyst
Prepared byIsrael Politics Analytic Team
Date29 September 2026
Versionv1.0

Information available as of 28 September 2026 was used in this assessment.

Scope Note

Question. Who is most likely to form Israel’s next government after the election to the 26th Knesset on 27 October 2026—and how confident can we be?

Timeframe. From the information cutoff (28 September 2026) through election day, the President’s consultations and government formation, including a possible repeat election, to mid-2027. The reference date for the premiership estimate is 1 June 2027.

Sourcing. 884 open sources: 466 Hebrew, 397 English and 21 Arabic (as tagged in our source register). They include the Central Elections Committee (CEC) timetable, plenary protocols and 29 raw poll filings; Supreme Court scheduling orders; Knesset records and consolidated Basic Laws; Hebrew outlets across the spectrum (Kan, N12, Channel 13, Channel 14, i24NEWS, Maariv, Ynet, Israel Hayom, Haaretz, Makor Rishon and Haredi media); Arabic-language media; survey institutes (IDI, INSS, JPPI, Dayan Center); and international wire and press reporting. Where an independent verifier corrected or refuted a collector’s finding, the verified version is used; claims we could not verify are labelled as such.

Methods. Poll aggregation (one poll per firm; right-leaning houses shown separately); a 100,000-draw Monte Carlo seat model with D’Hondt/Bader–Ofer allocation, threshold fade for new lists and Arab-list ban risk; coalition-formation analysis of recommendations and investiture votes; a key assumptions check; analysis of competing hypotheses; and a red-team review. Final scenario probabilities are the red team’s recommended figures, which reconcile three separately produced estimates that share one evidence base.

Out of scope. Preferences about who should win—our “position” is an analytic judgment of what will happen; detailed policy forecasts beyond what the evidence supports; non-public information; and events after 28 September 2026, including the Supreme Court rulings due 1–4 October.

Prepared by an independent open-source team to intelligence-community analytic standards (ICD 203). Not a product of any government agency.

Key Judgments

We assess that the 27 October election will likely (≈75–80%) produce a hung Knesset, in which neither Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu’s bloc nor the Zionist opposition reaches 61 seats without the other side or the Arab-majority lists. The latest poll from each of six mainstream firms (fieldwork 22–27 September) averages 51.2 seats for Netanyahu’s bloc including former general Ofer Winter’s Amcha Yisrael, 56.3 for the Zionist opposition including the centre-right Hendel–Zelekha list, and 12.5 for the Arab lists.1,2,3,4,5,6 Our three estimates put a hung Knesset at 74–79% (79% of draws in our 100,000-draw seat simulation); HaMadad’s model, which is not independent of Channel 13, puts “neither camp alone” at about 76%.7 Only the three right-leaning houses—Next Data for Channel 14 (63), Direct Polls for i24NEWS (60) and DRI (59)—put the bloc at or near 61.8,9,10 Our confidence is moderate because six firms agree, but they herd, disclose no weighting, and the right-leaning houses cannot be tested before the vote. Moderate confidence

We judge that deadlock followed by a repeat election in spring 2027 is the single most likely outcome (41%; plausible range 32–50%)—unlikely in absolute terms, but about twice as likely as any alternative. On mainstream numbers every route to investiture breaks at least one stated red line: Avigdor Liberman (Yisrael Beytenu) rules out a minority government (“we simply won’t be there”), Gadi Eisenkot (Yashar!) excludes Ra’am “at the moment” and rejects rotation, and Yoaz Hendel, co-leader of the Hendel–Zelekha list, rejects narrow coalitions and any that depend on Arab votes.11,12,13,14 Two of the four hung Knessets of 2019–21 went straight to repeat elections, although President Isaac Herzog now says he will do “everything” to prevent another.15,16 Our confidence in the ranking is moderate; our confidence in the size of the figure is lower, because it rests on red lines that held in 2019 but bent in 2020–21. Moderate confidence

We judge Netanyahu very unlikely—at the top of that band—to form a new government in this Knesset (19%), and an outright 61-seat majority for his bloc very unlikely (8–15% in our models; 10% that it wins 61 and forms a government). From the mainstream average, 61 requires a polling miss of about 10 seats, roughly three times the largest all-firm final-week underestimate of the right in 2019–22 (3.4 seats, in 2022).17,18,19 Statutory rotation is gone—Basic Law: The Government (Amendment No. 13) abolishes the alternate-prime-minister post from the 26th Knesset—and Eisenkot says rotation is “not on the table”.20,21,13 Our confidence is moderate because the judgment holds unless the right-leaning houses prove right, in which case the chance that Netanyahu forms a government would roughly double. Moderate confidence

We estimate that Netanyahu is likely, if only marginally, to still be prime minister on 1 June 2027 (56%; plausible range 46–65%)—in about two-thirds of those cases as head of a caretaker government during a deadlock, and in most of the rest as head of a new government he forms (S1–S2). The outgoing government serves until a new one is sworn in; a failed formation process runs the clock to a repeat election around March–May 2027; and from the 26th Knesset incapacity can be declared only on physical or mental grounds.22,23,24 No verdict in his trial is possible before the vote, and he says he will not step aside, “not for a moment”.25,26,27 Counting a 2027 rerun as roughly a coin flip between the camps, Netanyahu heads the next government actually sworn in with about 35–40% and Eisenkot with about 40–45%—neither is better than even; Polymarket traders on 27 September priced Eisenkot at 52.9% and Netanyahu at 29%.28 Our confidence is low because the figure turns on the rerun date and on red lines holding, neither testable before the vote. Low confidence

We judge Eisenkot more likely than Netanyahu to head any government formed in this Knesset (about 27% against 19%; about 47% against 33% if a government forms). The likeliest such government is a change government relying on Ra’am’s votes or abstention (21%) rather than a Zionist-only majority (13%). Yashar! leads the latest poll of six of nine firms, and Eisenkot leads Netanyahu head-to-head in Channel 13 (48–36), Maariv (47–39) and N12 (41–38), while Kan has Netanyahu ahead 39–38.1,4,29,30,31 Model estimates of the opposition plus Ra’am reaching 61 range from about 25–30% without Hendel–Zelekha, whose leaders reject reliance on Arab votes, to 42–47% with it; in 2021 a comparable government was sworn in 60–59.7,32 But former Prime Minister Naftali Bennett, who heads Together, and Liberman have declined to commit to Eisenkot as the bloc’s candidate before the vote.33,34,35 Our confidence is low because the judgment rests on post-election bargaining that polls cannot measure and on a leadership question the opposition has deferred. Low confidence

We assess that the pivotal uncertainty is whether stated red lines bend after the vote—above all Liberman’s double veto on any Netanyahu government and on any government that depends on Arab votes—a swing worth about ±10 points between a change government relying on Ra’am (S4) and deadlock followed by a rerun (S5). Liberman’s party voted on 23 September to bar Ra’am and the Joint List, yet he recommended Blue and White leader Benny Gantz alongside the Joint List in 2020 and served as finance minister in the 2021 government that Ra’am joined as a coalition partner (sworn in 60–59: three Ra’am MKs for, one abstaining).36,37,38,32,39 Eisenkot’s conditions are criteria Ra’am could in principle meet—his exclusion is hedged with “at the moment”—and Ra’am’s No. 2, Yoav Segalovitz, already calls Hamas a terror organisation that must be destroyed.12,40 Our confidence is low because there are only four linked precedents (2019–21), all involving the same actors. Low confidence

We judge it very likely that the Supreme Court reinstates the two Arab-majority lists, Ra’am (≈93%) and the Joint List (≈85%), by its 4 October deadline—both lists together about 80–85%. Only a ban that includes Ra’am (about 5–7%, almost always alongside a Joint List ban) would remove the route that relies on Ra’am’s votes and, on mainstream numbers, bring a Zionist-only change majority—not a Netanyahu majority—within reach; a Joint List-only ban (≈10%) would leave that route open. Court orders of 25 September set hearings for 1 October before nine justices under Supreme Court President Yitzhak Amit, without CEC chair Justice Noam Sohlberg, on the Joint List and on the bans on two of its candidates, MK Ofer Cassif and Balad chair Sami Abu Shehadeh; no hearing for Ra’am’s appeal had been published by 28 September.41,42 The court has reversed every CEC list ban since 2003, and Attorney General Gali Baharav-Miara opposed these bans.43,44 If both lists were barred and their voters stayed home, the mainstream average would give Netanyahu’s bloc 54–57 seats against 58–61 for the change bloc (62–65 with Hendel–Zelekha); only Channel 14 and i24NEWS numbers would put Netanyahu at 64–70.1,45,8 Our confidence is moderate because precedent and the Attorney General’s position point one way, but the Joint List’s exposure through Abu Shehadeh’s article of 8 October 2023 is untested. Moderate confidence

We judge a public dispute over the legitimacy of the result by at least one camp likely (60–70%); a contested result would lengthen formation and favour the caretaker. Both camps have pre-built grounds for rejecting a loss: coalition figures accuse the CEC and the courts of bias and blamed “foreign interference” for a report that the UAE warned Netanyahu before 7 October, while Arab and opposition leaders point to the list bans (“don’t let them steal your vote twice”, Ahmad Tibi of the Joint List told Arab voters).46,47,48,49,50 Asked how they would feel if their side lost, 81% of left-wing and 20% of right-wing respondents called it “intolerable”, and 56% of Israelis expect elements of the losing side to reject the result and use force.51,52 Networks that a private firm, Golden Owl, links to Iran—an attribution no Israeli agency has confirmed—are already seeding “stolen” and “postponed” narratives.53 The dispute would not rest on real fraud: fraud large enough to change the count has almost no chance (≤1%), because counting is decentralised and the CEC will publish per-station turnout and broadcast the central count of roughly 500,000 double-envelope ballots live.54 Our confidence is low on the dispute estimate, which depends on how close the result is, and high on the fraud judgment. Low confidence

Scenario Outlook: Deadlock Leads; Netanyahu Forms a Government in Fewer Than One Case in Five

We judge deadlock to be the modal outcome because it sits between two failure modes that point in opposite directions. If the mainstream polls overstate the anti-Netanyahu shift, the outcome moves toward S1 or S2; if pre-election red lines bend, it moves toward S4. Either break shrinks S5. The “Final” column adopts the red team’s recommended figures, which are small moves from the three estimator lenses; they should be read as ranges, not point estimates. On the ICD 203 scale every scenario is “unlikely” or “very unlikely” taken alone; S5 is nonetheless the single most likely outcome, at roughly twice the weight of S4.

The red team reclassified a unity government with Netanyahu serving first from S6 into S2, so the lenses’ S6 figures include items now counted in S2. All three lenses share one dossier, one poll baseline and one base rate; their agreement is a single estimate presented three times, not independent confirmation.

Eisenkot Likeliest to Form a Government in This Knesset; Netanyahu Likeliest to Hold Office on 1 June 2027

We judge that the question “who forms the next government” has two different answers depending on whether a 2027 rerun is counted. The derived quantities below follow directly from the scenario weights.

  • Competing hypotheses. Netanyahu-led government (S1+S2) 19%; opposition-led government (S3+S4) 34%; no new government or other (S5+S6) 47%. The lenses gave 16/32/52 (Quant), 18/33/49 (Coalition) and 20/36/44 (Contrarian).
  • Head of a government sworn in during this Knesset (unconditional). Eisenkot about 27%, Netanyahu about 19%, Bennett about 5%, a Likud successor about 3%, others about 4%; no new government (rerun or postponement) about 42%.
  • Repeat election. About 41% overall and about 53% conditional on a hung Knesset—slightly above the 2019–21 base rate (two of four hung Knessets, 50%) and well above a deadlock specialist’s 30–40%.
  • Netanyahu in office on 1 June 2027. 0.965×S1 + 0.94×S2 + 0.01×(S3+S4) + 0.88×S5 + 0.21×S6 ≈ 56% (range 46–65%). The S5 weight reflects that only an early (before mid-March) and decisive rerun installs a successor by June, since formation took 58–82 days in 2020–22; it also carries a small health or exit hazard. A rerun before mid-March would cut the figure by about 6 points; a May rerun would add about 3. In S6 only postponement keeps him in office.
  • Counting a 2027 rerun. Treating a rerun as roughly a coin flip between the camps, Netanyahu heads the next government actually sworn in with about 35–40% and Eisenkot with about 40–45% (27% in this Knesset, plus the opposition’s half of the 41% rerun branch times his four-fifths share of opposition-led governments); neither is better than even. Polymarket (27 September, via madad120) priced Eisenkot at 52.9%, Netanyahu 29%, Bennett 14% and Liberman 2.5%; that contract pays on whoever eventually becomes prime minister, including after a rerun, so the gap with our figure is smaller than it looks—but real.28

Three Estimator Lenses Agree—Because They Share One Evidence Base

We treat the close agreement of the three separately produced estimates as one judgment presented three times rather than as corroboration. Each lens worked from the same verified dossier, the same canonical poll baseline and the same 2019–21 base rate, and none ran new research.

Electoral Mechanics and Calendar: Rules Are Fixed; the Supreme Court Ruling Is the Last Rules-Driven Swing Factor

We judge it almost certain (≥97%) that voting takes place on Tuesday 27 October 2026 as scheduled. The date—16 Heshvan 5787—is the statutory end-of-term date, and the dissolution clause, anchored in a party-financing law, passed its final readings overnight on 16–17 July 2026 with 62 votes in favour and none against.60,61,62,63 It is the first Knesset election held on its original date since 1988.64 Extending the Knesset’s term requires a law passed by 80 MKs and “special circumstances” that prevent an election (Basic Law: The Knesset §9A); the only national precedent is 1973, no bill exists, and Justice Minister Yariv Levin said in May that postponement is “not on the agenda”.59,65,66 The CEC can only delay, pause, relocate or extend voting locally, by up to 24 hours past the 22:00 close.67

How Israel’s System Works

  • One national constituency. All 120 seats are filled from closed party lists. A list must win 3.25% of valid votes—about 154,000–171,000 votes at 65–72% turnout of 7,331,981 eligible voters, which in practice yields at least about four seats.68,69
  • Seat allocation. Seats are allocated by the D’Hondt (Bader–Ofer) method. Two lists may sign a surplus-vote agreement, filed by 16 October and published 19 October; it counts only if both lists pass the threshold and is worth at most about one seat.60,70
  • The mandate. Official results are due 4 November and the new Knesset first sits on 10 November. Within seven days of the results the President consults every faction and tasks the MK with the best chance of forming a government—in 2019–22 practice, the candidate with the most recommendations. The nominee has 28 days plus up to 14; a second MK can get 28 days; a majority of MKs can then name a candidate within 21 days. If all rounds fail, the Knesset is deemed dissolved and elections are held on the last Tuesday before day 90.22,56,71,15
  • Investiture. A government is constituted by a Knesset vote of confidence decided by a majority of those voting; abstentions do not count, so 61 supporters are not required.22,39
  • Caretaker rule. The outgoing government continues in office until a new one is sworn in, under a legal duty of restraint that tightens after an election.22,24
  • No statutory rotation. The alternate-prime-minister (rotation) government in §13A is repealed from the start of the 26th Knesset (passed 84–0 in May 2025). An indicted MK can serve as prime minister but not as an ordinary minister, so Netanyahu cannot wait inside a government for a turn.20,21,72

We assess that the field is structured around a few large alliances, several of them built to split after the vote. Thirty-eight lists were filed on 7–8 September.73,74 Bennett 2026 and Yesh Atid run as Together (ביחד), a joint list of two registered parties; Religious Zionism runs with Zehut as a technical bloc; Likud and New Hope filed jointly without a formal merger; the Joint List reunites Hadash, Ta’al and Balad without Ra’am; and United Torah Judaism is a three-party submission that includes a faction registered so that it can break away.75,76,77,78,79

We judge the Supreme Court very likely to reinstate Ra’am (≈93%) and the Joint List (≈85%), likely (≈75–80%) to reinstate MK Ofer Cassif, and a roughly even chance (≈50%; one of our verifiers put it at 55–65%) to uphold the personal ban on Balad chair Sami Abu Shehadeh. On 23 September the CEC plenum barred Ra’am (18–5–1 on each of three grounds, on petitions by Otzma Yehudit, Likud and a bereaved-families forum) and the Joint List (18–5–1 on each of two grounds, on Likud’s petition); it barred Cassif 19–5–2 and Abu Shehadeh 30–4 on one ground and 31–4 on another, the latter with CEC chair Justice Noam Sohlberg voting in favour—the first chair to vote since 2003.80,81,44 Ra’am was barred on all three statutory grounds (negating Israel as a Jewish and democratic state, incitement to racism, and support for armed struggle), the Joint List on the first and third. The case against Abu Shehadeh rests mainly on an Arabic article of 8 October 2023 that called the Hamas attack “an important historic event militarily, politically and strategically”; he says he has “never called for violence or terrorism” and opposes armed struggle. The votes crossed blocs: Yisrael Beytenu voted to bar both lists, Together’s members stayed out of the list votes and voted to bar Abu Shehadeh, and every Zionist opposition party backed his ban—Yashar!, which has no committee seat, by statement.81,82,83,84,44,85,36,86 On 24 September petitions to bar Otzma Yehudit (10–19–1), Religious Zionism–Zehut (3–21) and the Democrats (2–14; the other coalition factions stayed out of the vote, as MK Simcha Rothman announced on their behalf) failed; the Attorney General’s representative called Otzma Yehudit’s case “dangerously close” to the line but not over it.87,44 The Attorney General opposed both list bans and the Cassif ban but found “a basis for disqualification” of Abu Shehadeh.44,88 Court orders of 25 September signed by Supreme Court President Yitzhak Amit set hearings for 1 October before nine justices, without Sohlberg, on the Joint List and on Cassif and Abu Shehadeh; no hearing or case number for Ra’am’s appeal had been published by 28 September. Appeals were due by 29 September, and rulings are due by 4 October.41,42,60 Since 2003 the court has reversed at least one CEC ban in every election but one, and it reversed the list bans of 2009, 2019 and 2022.43,89,90 The Joint List is more exposed than Ra’am, because Abu Shehadeh’s article of 8 October 2023 may be imputed to it and two justices left that door open in 2022; Kan’s legal sources expect Abu Shehadeh’s ban to be upheld “exceptionally”, while IDI notes the court has never upheld a CEC ban on a candidate.91,92,81,93

We judge that threshold outcomes will matter more than surplus deals or withdrawals. In 2022 a record 8.76% of valid votes (417,400 of 4,764,742) went to lists below the threshold, most of them on the anti-Netanyahu side.94,95,96 Surplus agreements between Yashar! and the Democrats, Together and Yisrael Beytenu, and Ra’am and the Joint List are signed; Likud–RZ–Zehut and Shas–UTJ are reported both as signed and as not formally completed; Otzma Yehudit, Amcha Yisrael, Hendel–Zelekha and Blue and White have no partner.97,98,99,100,101 The Elections Law sets no deadline for a list to withdraw; the practical window closes when lists are published on 18 October, and Ofer Winter says it is “too late” for Amcha Yisrael to quit.102,103

We assess turnout as a larger swing variable in the Arab sector than nationally. National turnout ran between 67.44% and 71.52% in 2019–22 (70.63% in 2022); turnout in Arab and Druze localities swung from 64.8% (2020) to 44.6% (2021) and 53.2% (2022).104,105,94,106 Roughly 500,000 double-envelope ballots—soldiers, patients, diplomats and, this time, evacuees—are counted centrally about two days after polling and can flip threshold calls.54,107

Seat Arithmetic: Netanyahu’s Bloc Short of a Majority in Every Poll but Channel 14’s

We assess that on the mainstream average Netanyahu’s bloc stands about ten seats short of 61, the Zionist opposition about eight short (about five with Hendel–Zelekha), and the Arab lists hold the balance. Terms: “Zionist opposition” and “change bloc” (the opposition’s own label since 2021) both mean Yashar!, Together, Yisrael Beytenu and the Democrats, plus Hendel–Zelekha where stated. “Mainstream” and “right-leaning” group pollsters by measured lean, not by quality; DRI is grouped with Next Data and Direct Polls on its results (59 seats), because its two polls are too few to measure a house effect.10 The latest poll from each of six mainstream firms puts the five coalition parties—Likud (הליכוד), Shas (ש״ס), United Torah Judaism (UTJ; יהדות התורה), Otzma Yehudit (עוצמה יהודית) and Religious Zionism–Zehut (RZ–Zehut)—at 47.0 seats, 51.2 with Ofer Winter’s Amcha Yisrael (עמך ישראל); the four Zionist opposition lists at 53.0, 56.3 with the Hendel–Zelekha list; and the Arab lists at 12.5.1,30,45,4,6,5 The three right-leaning houses average 59.3, 60.7 with Amcha. We judge that the roughly 12-seat gap between the two groups of pollsters on the five coalition parties (about 10 with Amcha) is larger than any threshold, turnout or Arab-list effect in play.

We judge that the largest-party race is close and pollster-dependent, but that it matters less than the bloc arithmetic. Yashar! (ישר!) leads Likud 22.7 to 19.5 in the mainstream average; the right-leaning firms reverse the order, 28.3 to 20.7, and a simple five-poll average that includes Channel 14 and i24NEWS puts Likud narrowly ahead, 23.0 to 22.2.123 Our model has Yashar! ahead in 58% of draws, tied in 9% and behind in 33%. The President assigns the mandate by recommendations, not by the size of the largest list.

Momentum: Movement Inside the Anti-Netanyahu Camp, Not Between Blocs

We assess that the big movement since April happened inside the anti-Netanyahu camp, from the Bennett–Lapid list to Eisenkot’s Yashar!, while the coalition eroded only slowly. Since late August the mainstream Zionist opposition has lost four to five seats while Amcha (launched 25 August), Hendel–Zelekha and the re-formed Joint List gained; this is an inference from net seat changes, not a measured flow, and Israel Hayom reports that Winter draws mainly from Likud.124,125

  • Together fell from 26 seats (N12, 27 April) to 11–13 in mainstream polls and 7–9 in the right-leaning houses.130,123

Threshold Risk Runs Both Ways This Time

We judge that, unlike 2022, the risk of wasted votes falls on both camps, so threshold outcomes alone are very unlikely to hand either bloc 61. Each list that fails moves two to four seats, but on the equal-weight average of the latest CEC filings none of the 16 pass/fail combinations of Amcha, Hendel–Zelekha, RZ–Zehut and Ra’am gives either bloc a majority: Netanyahu’s bloc lands at 51–58, the Jewish change bloc with Hendel–Zelekha at 51–58, and the Arab lists at 9–13.131

  • History. In 2022, 6.1 points of wasted vote fell on the anti-Netanyahu side (Meretz 3.16%, Balad 2.91%) against about 1.2 on the right, turning a vote edge of about 30,000—under one seat—into a 64-seat majority.96,95 All six new or soft near-threshold lists of 2019–22 fell short of their final polls (mean −1.1 points); all five Arab lists matched or beat theirs (mean +0.6).136,137,138,139
  • Softness. In Kantar’s 9 September filing only 12% of Amcha voters were completely sure of their vote and 42% were not so sure; in Kantar’s 16–17 September filing 15% of all respondents had changed their vote since the lists closed.140,133
  • Expected waste. About 6.5–7% of valid votes, split roughly evenly (≈3.2 points on Netanyahu’s side, ≈3.4 among change and unaligned lists). Both Amcha and Hendel–Zelekha failing (likely, ≈60%) pushes waste to about 9%; waste turns lopsided only if exactly one fails (≈20–35%), which moves the balance by three to four seats.
  • If the Arab lists were barred. The arithmetic is in the Key Judgments; earlier estimates placing Netanyahu “near 61” after a ban rested on including the right-leaning houses or on misreading N12, whose coalition figure is 46 (50 with Amcha), not 54.2,30

House Effects: Two Right-Leaning Houses Lean 7–12 Seats, and the Gap Sits in Raw Likud Share

We judge that the mainstream firms set the centre of the distribution, and we give the right-leaning picture 10–20% weight—a weight that cannot be tested before 27 October and that drives almost all of S1. Measured against the same-week mean of the other mainstream houses across 241 polls since 7 May, two houses carry large pro-coalition effects and every other house sits within about ±2 seats.124

  • Raw shares, not seat conversion. CEC filings show raw Likud support of 23–24% for Next Data and 20–22% for Direct Polls against 14–17% for Kantar, Midgam, Lazar and Maagar Mochot; undecided shares run 2–4% (Next Data) against 9–11% (Midgam). No house discloses its weighting targets, so method and bias cannot be separated from open sources.132,141,9,2,135,140
  • Transparency and ownership. Channel 14’s filings show response rates of about 1–4% (about 10% in one); the CEC asked Channel 14 for corrections to its filings, as it had Channel 13 and i24NEWS.131,132,141,142 TheMarker reports that Next Data is held by Ofarim Media, controlled by Channel 14’s owner; Haaretz reports that DRI belongs to the S.M.L.T. consultancy, which ran internal polls for Likud—the company says its polls are independent.143,144 Channel 14 rejects the criticism and says its polls “spot trends weeks or months before competitors”.145
  • Track record. The 2022 Filber–Direct Polls partnership was the least-wrong house in 2022 (final poll 62 against a result of 64), but it split in 2025; neither current house carries that record intact, and today’s leans are 3.5–6 times the roughly +2 relative lean of 2022.17,146
  • Herding. From June to mid-August every house’s poll-to-poll standard deviation on the coalition was 0.5–1.2 seats, against 1.9–2.7 expected from sampling alone; a stable series is not evidence of accuracy for any house.124

Historical Poll Error: A Modest Correction Toward the Right, Not a 61-Seat Bloc

We judge that history supports a modest correction toward the right—about +1 to +2 seats on the five coalition parties, concentrated on Shas—not taking Channel 14’s 63 as the central case. Reaching 61 from the mainstream 51.2 needs +9.8 seats, about 2.9 times the largest all-firm final-week underestimate of the right (3.4 seats in 2022; the one larger error, +3.6 in September 2019, overstated the right), though over the whole 2022 campaign polls underestimated the coalition by 5.68 seats.17 The tails are nonetheless fatter than the 2019–22 errors imply: late consolidation (Likud in April 2019) and new-party fades (New Hope in 2021) are both live, about ten seats’ worth of 2022 coalition voters now sit with a first-time party or among the undecided, and the poll blackout from the end of Friday 23 October hides the final four days.114

Political Climate and Issue Salience: No Issue Is Moving Voters Between Blocs

We judge that no single issue is moving voters between blocs. Security leads the agenda but has produced no lasting rally; 7 October accountability and the Haredi draft are the opposition’s strongest frames; the economy and international pressure are close to neutral in seat terms. Because the policy centre of gravity is hawkish, the contest turns on competence, accountability and leadership rather than on direction.

Source: IDI Israeli Voice Index, fieldwork 31 August–3 September 2026 (605 Hebrew and 150 Arabic interviews); share naming each factor first or second; — = not reported. Among Jews, the self-described right names security most often (47%), the centre the economy (40.5%) and then the draft (34%), and the left religion and state (39%); about a quarter of Haredim call 7 October central.52,152 Rankings are wording-sensitive. Kan (12 July) asked what will decide the election: security 25%, 7 October and the inquiry 24%, economy 22%, judicial reform 12%. JPPI’s single-choice question (2–5 August) put security first (31%) and government–judiciary relations second (17%). A single-issue question in Channel 14/Next Data’s 15 September filing put judicial reform first (25%), ahead of 7 October and the identity of the prime minister (17% each), war and security (13%), the draft (10%) and cost of living (9%).153,154,141

Security: Salient, but Not a Clean Asset for Netanyahu

We assess that no front will be settled before the vote, the Iran war produced no victory narrative, and the hawkish consensus denies the opposition a policy contrast; the net effect is a mild headwind for the incumbent, and late shocks are likelier to reshuffle votes within the right than between blocs.

  • Iran. The US–Israeli war began on 28 February 2026 (Israel’s Operation Roaring Lion); the opening strikes killed Supreme Leader Ali Khamenei, who was succeeded by his son Mojtaba.155,156,157 A 7–8 April ceasefire gave way to a June memorandum under which the US lifted its blockade on 18 June; Trump declared the ceasefire over on 8 July and the blockade was reimposed on 14 July. Israel and Iran last exchanged fire directly on 7–8 June.157,158 US and Iranian officials met on the sidelines of the UN General Assembly on 22 September, with accounts differing on whether the talks were direct or mediated; Trump then rejected Iran’s seven-day plan to reopen Hormuz (“They made a proposal but I rejected it”), and the Wall Street Journal reports that he expects to resume bombing after the 3 November midterms.159,160,161,162,116 Brent crude stayed above $105 a barrel.163
  • Gaza and the Yellow Line. The 10 October 2025 ceasefire holds loosely: the IDF controls about 60% or more of the Strip behind the Yellow Line, Hamas has not disarmed, and neither the international stabilisation force nor the technocratic committee has deployed.164,165 Netanyahu told the cabinet on 9 August that Israel rejects the 15-point disarmament document; the Board of Peace unveiled a six-month, $2.45bn, 66-project plan on 23 September.166,167,168 On 22 September Defence Minister Israel Katz threatened that if a soldier or Israeli civilian is kidnapped, Gaza City “will be treated the way Rafah, Beit Hanoun and 70% of Gaza’s territory were treated”.169,170,171 UNRWA, citing the Gaza Health Ministry, counts 1,394 Palestinians killed between the ceasefire and 20 September; AP gives 1,145 (unreconciled) and reports seven IDF soldiers killed.172,173
  • Hostages. All living hostages returned in October 2025 and the remains of the last, Ran Gvili, were recovered on 26 January 2026; the main mobilising issue of 2024–25 is closed, although Katz’s threat shows it could return.174,175
  • Lebanon. Hezbollah joined the war on 2 March; Israel holds a self-declared security zone about 10 km deep, which a Prime Minister’s Office–Defence Ministry statement of 10 September called consolidated at the Ali al-Taher ridge.176,177 A June framework ties phased withdrawal to Hezbollah’s disarmament; talks were postponed and are to resume in October, and Hezbollah attacks IDF troops inside Lebanon rather than Israeli towns.178,179,180
  • West Bank. OCHA data to 4 September, as cited by the Times of India, record more than 1,430 settler incidents in 2026, in which more than 1,100 Palestinians were injured and 23 killed, and more than 2,500 Palestinians displaced; tenders for about 1,200 homes in E1 close before the vote.181,182,183 Over January 2025–July 2026 OCHA counts 316 Palestinians killed by Israeli forces or settlers and 20 Israelis killed by Palestinians; attacks on Israelis continued in late September, with a fatal shooting at Neve Tzuf and a ramming that gravely wounded Neria Leiter.182,184,185 On Ynet’s “120 ve-Achat” podcast (26–27 September) Finance Minister Bezalel Smotrich said Israel must “go to war in Judea and Samaria, to do there what we did in Gaza” and dismantle the Palestinian Authority.186,76 We found no formal annexation law passed in 2026.
  • Opinion. In June 15% said Israel won the Iran war, 37% Iran and 43% neither (INSS, 16–22 June, n=957).187,188 Majorities opposed the April truce (Kan 56%, Channel 12 53%, Channel 13 51%) and Likud lost two to three seats afterwards, although Kan’s pre-ceasefire poll had shown a temporary bump to 28.189,190 In September only 27% said Israel won in Gaza and 23% against Iran, and 59% think a 7 October-type attack could recur.191,192 Jewish opinion is hawkish: 72% of Jews would not agree “under any condition” to withdraw from the Gaza security perimeter, and 72% of all Israelis want a permanent security zone in southern Lebanon.193,194 Naftali Bennett’s security doctrine (19 August) promises strikes inside Iran in response to Hezbollah fire and, according to Arab48, no withdrawal from the Yellow Line before Hamas is fully disarmed.195,196

We judge that security news mostly reinforces existing blocs, because whether Israel is “winning” has become a partisan marker (JPPI, September: 77% on the right, 16% in the centre, 0% on the left).193 In 2015, sirens in the final days added 2.6 points to Likud in exposed localities, taken from other right-wing lists, with turnout unchanged.197 A late barrage would therefore be worth roughly one to two seats to Likud and no more than one to the bloc; a US–Iran round after 3 November would land inside coalition formation and favour the caretaker.

7 October Accountability: The Opposition’s Strongest Frame, Worth About One Seat at the Anniversary

We assess that accountability is the opposition’s strongest frame with centre and centre-right Jewish voters and a main reason Netanyahu’s bloc stays below 61 in mainstream polls, but that anniversary salience (3 October by the Hebrew date, 7 October civil) is unlikely to add more than about one seat.

  • 68% of Jews call 7 October central to their vote; 75% support a state commission of inquiry, including 52% of coalition voters (INSS, 9–14 September).52,191,192 Support is wording-sensitive: 55% named a state commission the most appropriate body in IDI’s December 2025 survey, down about 10 points, with a roughly 20-point fall on the right.198,199 62% rate Netanyahu’s conduct on the morning of 7 October “not good” (Kan, 6 September).200
  • Haaretz reported on 8 September that UAE President Mohammed bin Zayed warned Netanyahu of a Hamas plan in late September 2023 and that he did not brief his security chiefs; the Prime Minister’s Office called it a “complete lie”.201,202,203 The New York Times reported on 25 September that the warning lacked specifics and never reached the chiefs, and The Atlantic reported a secret warning visit by Egypt’s intelligence chief Abbas Kamel on 26 September 2023, which the Prime Minister’s Office denies.204,205 Netanyahu met bin Zayed in Abu Dhabi on 27 September; AP’s sources say he asked him to deny the report, while N12 says the talks focused on Iran—the purpose is contested.206,207
  • No state commission exists. The coalition’s alternative bill, under which the coalition and the opposition would each appoint three members, passed a first reading 59–0 during an opposition boycott and was not completed before dissolution; the Attorney General proposed on 19 August that the next government decide, and the High Court will not rule on the petitions before the vote.208,209,210,211 Eisenkot, Bennett, Liberman and Yair Golan, leader of the Democrats, pledged jointly that a state commission “will be established immediately”.203

Haredi Draft: The Sharpest Wedge, and a Bind for the Haredi Parties

We judge the draft to be the opposition’s sharpest wedge among reservists and secular and traditional voters and, structurally, a bind for the Haredi parties: they have almost no chance (≤5%) of enabling anyone but Netanyahu, and on mainstream numbers they cannot make him prime minister.

  • Legislation. No enlistment law passed. Basic Law: Torah Study passed on 13 July (63–52; a petition is pending).212,213,214 A law freezing arrests of yeshiva students without a deferral, whom the IDF classes as draft evaders, until 30 November passed on 14 July (58–54), was frozen the next day by Justice Ofer Grosskopf and was voided on 3 September (9–0 on procedure, 8–1 on equality, Justice Sohlberg writing).215,216,217 The court recorded 219 arrests among about 73,664 evaders; police and the IDF reportedly halted proactive arrests in August, which the IDF disputes.216,218,219
  • Opinion. The arrest-freeze law had 25% support and 67% opposition (IDI, 28 July–2 August); 63% of Jews opposed the enforcement freeze, including 62% of traditional Jews (INSS, July).220,221 About 70% of reservists say the government’s handling of the draft affects their vote to a large or very large extent (Dialog for Yedioth/Ynet, 19 August, n=509), and the draft is “significant” to the vote for 80% of Jewish opposition voters against 33% of coalition voters (Channel 13, 29 May).222,223
  • Positions. Winter will not enter a government unless a draft law passes before it is formed; Bennett says “whoever doesn’t enlist won’t get a shekel”; Yisrael Beytenu demands a draft law with no quotas, targets or exemptions; Eisenkot proposes universal service with up to 3% of each cohort deferred, which Shas leader Aryeh Deri has called worse than Bennett’s plan.55,224,34,225,226,227 At Degel HaTorah’s 26 September rally Rabbi Moshe Hillel Hirsch’s message called the election “פיקוח נפש”, a matter of life and death; Elyakim Stark (Belz; UTJ #6) said, “we did not manage to protect the yeshiva students”.228,229,230

Trial, Pardon and the Judiciary: Relevant After the Vote, Not Before

We judge legal developments very unlikely to change the pre-vote picture; their relevance comes afterwards, where a plea deal or pardon could enter deadlock bargaining—though an exit deal within the formation window is very unlikely (5–15%).

  • Trial. Netanyahu finished testifying in June after 98 hearing days; defence witnesses continue, with hearings five days a week from 4 October, so no verdict is possible before the vote.25,26
  • Pardon. His November 2025 request is pending. The Justice Ministry’s pardons department found that it does not meet the threshold conditions because there has been no conviction, and the file moved from Levin to Minister Amichai Eliyahu in March 2026.231 President Herzog told Channel 13 on 16 September that the case “must end, preferably in a plea deal”: he offered mediation months ago, the Attorney General accepted, Netanyahu has not answered, and Herzog says he has “unlimited authority” on pardons; the same day he told Channel 14 that a resolution “won’t happen during the elections”.232,233,234 The Attorney General insists that any deal carry moral turpitude.58
  • Signals. On 19 September Herzog cleared the criminal record of former soldier Elor Azaria on Katz’s recommendation, despite an earlier IDF opinion against it, and National Security Minister Itamar Ben Gvir urged the same for Netanyahu; Trump has publicly pressed Herzog.235,236,232 The Qatargate and leaked-document probes of Netanyahu’s aides remain pre-indictment; we judge an indictment before the vote very unlikely (10–20%).237
  • Judicial confrontation. Levin refuses to convene the judicial selection committee while four Supreme Court seats are vacant; the cabinet voted on 5 July not to recognise a High Court interim order on the broadcast regulator; the court voided the Attorney General’s dismissal in December 2025; and the 2025 judicial-selection amendment takes effect with the next Knesset.66,238,239,240 Smotrich promises “ten Supreme Court justices … a new AG” next term.241 The issue has low salience as a voting factor (18% of Jews) but high stakes for the legitimacy of the result.

Economy and Cost of Living: A Benign Backdrop That Moves No More Than One or Two Seats

We judge it very likely (90–95%) that economic news moves no more than one or two seats before the vote. The Bank of Israel cut its rate to 3.25% on 1 September, its fourth cut of 2026 and third in a row; annual inflation was 1.5% in August; unemployment is 3.3%; and the 12-month deficit was 3.2% of GDP against a 4.9% ceiling.113,242,243,244 GDP fell 3.8% (annualised) in the war quarter and rose 15.4% in the second; first-half growth of 3.2% drops to about 1% excluding Nvidia, and private consumption fell 0.4%.245,246 The 2026 budget (NIS 850.6bn) carries a record NIS 143bn for defence, a 3% cut to civilian ministries and NIS 5.17bn for Haredi institutions; the Treasury’s first estimate of the Iran war’s cost is NIS 35bn.247,248 Food prices are up 8% since 2024, rents for new tenants rose 4.4%, and reservists face an average long-term salary loss of 4.7%.249,242 Cost of living is a top-two concern (38% of Jews; 40.5% in the centre), but IDI’s Tamar Hermann sees no “difference in the economic agenda of the major parties”, so no party owns the issue; grievance reaches the ballot mainly through the “equal burden” frame.52,249 The Governor expects inflation to rise toward 2%, and markets see a hold at the decision just before the vote.250

International Pressure and UN General Assembly Week: Pressure Consolidates the Right More Than It Moves Swing Voters

We judge Western pressure to be real and escalating but very unlikely to shift the bloc balance; the government frames it as election interference, and opposition leader Yair Lapid has called sanctions “a grave mistake” (Al Jazeera; not independently verified).251

  • Measures. On 8 September twelve states (the UK, France, Canada, Denmark, Finland, Iceland, Ireland, Norway, Poland, Portugal, Spain and Sweden) declared their intention to introduce or support restrictions on settlement goods; only the UK, France and Canada committed to national bans, and the UK’s legislation is six to nine months away.252,253,254 The UK now refuses arms licences for exports that “materially contribute to the occupation” and added five settler sanctions designations; a cumulative 38 is cited by the House of Commons Library but not independently verified.182,255 Foreign Minister Gideon Sa’ar retaliated on 8 September—closing the UK consulate in East Jerusalem within 30 days, expelling UK officers from the Kiryat Gat coordination centre, halting UK training of Palestinian Authority forces and barring 11 or 12 UK elected officials (sources differ)—and called the move interference “in the election campaign of a sovereign state”.254,256 The EU-27 remain divided; EU foreign policy chief Kaja Kallas backed a bloc-wide ban on 10–11 September. US Secretary of State Marco Rubio said, “We are not going to do what Britain did,” although Washington did not condemn London.257,258,259
  • UN General Assembly week (22–24 September). UN Secretary-General António Guterres warned of the “specter of ethnic cleansing”; French President Emmanuel Macron called the situation “a spectacle that shames us all”; UK Prime Minister Andy Burnham criticised Israeli conduct in Gaza and the West Bank while saying Britain stands with Israel against Iran and Hamas; Turkish President Recep Tayyip Erdoğan, Jordan’s King Abdullah II, Qatar’s emir and Brazilian President Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva also criticised Israel. Dozens of delegates walked out as Netanyahu began speaking on 24 September.260,261,262 From the podium Netanyahu said, “If there are any other moral cowards who haven’t left this hall, please do so now,” and called genocide claims “the biggest lie of the century”; his office called Macron’s claim that Hamas had “never” operated in the West Bank a “grotesque absurdity” (a French source said the word was a mistranslation of “governed”), and both Macron and Burnham also condemned Hamas and the 7 October attack.262,263,261,260 Trump hailed his Gaza plan and threatened to “annihilate” Iran.264 The ICC warrant for Netanyahu remains in force; prosecutor Karim Khan was removed on 24 July, and the US sanctioned ICC officials in August.265,266,267
  • Opinion. A median 67% across 36 countries view Israel unfavourably (Pew).268 In Israel, 62% are worried about the country’s standing and 31% are not; 88% of opposition voters are worried against 36% of coalition voters, 59% of whom are not worried (Maariv, 23–24 September); 71% say Netanyahu’s UN speech will not affect their vote (Kan, 27 September); and 57% of Jews say Israel should change nothing because of rising antisemitism (JPPI).45,119,29,1,193 Lapid calls sanctions “a grave mistake”, and pollster Dahlia Scheindlin sees “no real backlash” (both reported by Al Jazeera; not independently verified).251

Parties and Blocs: The Anti-Netanyahu Camp Leads or Ties in Six of Nine Pollsters but Is Split on Its Leader and Its Partners

We assess that two features of the party system matter most for government formation. The anti-Netanyahu camp is divided over leadership (Eisenkot, Bennett, Liberman) and over whether Arab or Haredi parties can be partners; and several joint lists on both sides are built to split after the vote, which creates marginal pivots. Only Religious Zionism–Zehut is unconditionally committed to Netanyahu; the Haredi parties are publicly uncommitted.16,269 Poll averages below are the six mainstream firms and the three right-leaning firms (latest poll per firm).

Mergers and Splits: Joint Lists on Both Sides Are Built to Split After the Vote

  • Together (26 April 2026) is a joint list of two registered parties, with Yesh Atid holding about 40% of slots, that can split back into two after the vote. It peaked at 25–26 seats and now polls 11–13; several Yesh Atid MKs left, and Segalovitz is now Ra’am’s No. 2.308,75,289,304
  • Yashar! refused a merger with Bennett (Eisenkot: “no intention of being No. 2”, 28 March). Tropper’s Zionist Home joined it as a joint list on 6 September, after which Hendel paired with Zelekha; Gilad Erdan and Yuli Edelstein’s Unity list withdrew on 4 September.309,310,311
  • Religious Zionism and Zehut agreed a technical bloc on 1 September. N12 says Netanyahu supported it in principle; Reuters reported his active help—the accounts conflict. Religious Zionism polled at or below the threshold before the merger.312,313
  • UTJ is one list of three parties; Chomat Torat Yisrael (Meir Porush #4, Elyakim Stark #6) was registered so that it can break away, and veterans Moshe Gafni (#120) and Uri Maklev (#118) were moved to symbolic slots.79,314
  • The Joint List reunited Hadash, Ta’al and Balad on 19 August without Ra’am, which had wanted only a technical bloc; the two signed a surplus-vote agreement on the night of 11 September.78,99
  • Opposition coordination. On 26 September Lapid, Eisenkot, Bennett, Liberman and Golan signed a principles document at Lapid’s home: non-aggression, joint turnout work and a team to draft guidelines including a state commission of inquiry and a prime-ministerial term limit. It names no candidate for prime minister, deferring the question until after the vote, and Gantz was not invited.315,316,317,318 A post-election Bennett–Liberman technical merger to overtake Yashar! has been floated in N12 analysis.291

We judge the 26 September pact to be coordination, not a merger. The bloc is split on Arab partners (Golan against the rest), on Haredi partners (Together and Yisrael Beytenu set conditions the Haredim reject; Eisenkot is conditional) and on leadership (three contenders). With split points built into lists on both sides, marginal pivots after the vote are likelier than in 2022.

Public Sentiment: Anti-Incumbent on Performance, Hawkish on Policy

We assess that Israeli opinion in 2026 is anti-incumbent on performance and accountability but hawkish on policy. Trust in the government is about a quarter and in Netanyahu about a third, and most voters said he should not run again—yet he remains the single most preferred prime minister when several names are offered, and the electorate’s policy centre sits right of centre. The swing lies with centre-right Jews, reservists, traditional Likud voters and Arab turnout.

Trust and Leadership: Low Trust, Yet Netanyahu Still the Most Preferred Single Leader

  • Trust. INSS (9–14 September, n=920) finds high trust in the government at 27%, in Netanyahu at 31% (Jews 37%, Arabs 5%; coalition voters 67%, opposition voters 5%) and in the IDF at 73%.191 Levels depend on the scale: Reichman University’s July index put the government at 18% and the Knesset at 12%, while JPPI found in August that 38% rely on the prime minister and 50% “not at all”.319,154,320 Any wartime rally was small and short-lived: INSS’s monthly series had trust in Netanyahu at 36% in January and 31% from June through September.191,221
  • Netanyahu’s future. IDI (31 May–5 June) found 61% saying he should not run (Jews 57%, Arabs 83%), including about two-thirds of the self-described centre-right and about a quarter of 2022 Likud, Religious Zionism and UTJ voters.321,322 Maariv (23–24 September) finds 34% wanting him as prime minister after the election, including 82% of coalition voters.45,119
  • Preference for prime minister. With several names offered, JPPI (1–3 September) has Netanyahu 36% (Jews 43%), Eisenkot 29%, Bennett 13% and Liberman 4%.193,323 Head-to-head against Eisenkot, Netanyahu trails in Channel 13 (36–48, 23 September), Maariv (39–47) and N12 (38–41, 22 September), leads narrowly in Kan (39–38, 27 September) and leads in Channel 14’s forced choice (53–47).4,29,30,31,8 He leads Bennett (N12 41–37; Kan 42–33) and Liberman (N12 38–28).30,31
  • Mood and stakes. 61% call the national mood poor (Jews 65.5%, down from 73% a year earlier); Jewish optimism has risen, driven by the right (43% say Israel is improving, from 36%).52,152,193 40% call this the most important election in the state’s history—55–60% in the centre and on the left, against about 35% on the right.323
  • Integrity perceptions. 70% see a danger to the election’s integrity (Jews 72%; among Jews, left 90%, centre 76%, right 66%). 59% expect Israeli political actors, and 51% foreign actors, to try to sway the result improperly, and 56% expect some on the losing side to refuse the result and use force.324,220,52
  • Policy centre. 17% of Israelis think a Palestinian state could coexist peacefully with Israel (Pew). Among Jews, 38% favour annexing the whole West Bank against 35% for partition, and the share choosing “strengthen control, expand settlements, perhaps annex” rose from 38% (2024) to 48% (2026).325,154,320

Swing Constituencies: Centre-Right Jews and Arab Turnout Decide the Margin

  • Centre-right Jews are the only genuinely contested camp: their preference for prime minister splits Netanyahu 44%, Eisenkot 21%, Bennett 20%; about two-thirds said in June that he should not run; 68% wanted an immediate inquiry in May; and only 55% were fully decided in August.193,322,51,154
  • The undecided pool is 11.5%, but only 1.2% are torn between blocs (N12, 7 September), so late movement will mostly be within blocs and will decide the largest party more than the bloc balance.128
  • Stated turnout (N12, 7 September): 69% overall; coalition voters 75%, opposition voters 81%, third-bloc voters 61%, Arab-party voters 53%, undecided 38%.128 A Kan figure of 45% of Arab voters “sure to vote” circulates via madad120 but is not in Kan’s published article; self-reported turnout is inflated in any case.28,1
  • Arab turnout. The Dayan Center’s May projection is 53%, rising to 67% only with a four-party list that does not exist this time; Channel 13’s Arab-sector partner reportedly assumes about 60%; the one post-ban reading points down; and Arab leaders are calling for counter-mobilisation—Ahmad Tibi: “don’t let them steal your vote twice”—with no boycott call found.331,338,339,332,50 We use a range of 48–55%.
  • The differential. The stakes asymmetry and stated intent favour opposition turnout, but 2022 is the counter-precedent: right-wing turnout and a Haredi rise produced a 64-seat majority from a vote edge of under one seat.96

Key Actors and Stakeholders: A Few Veto Players, Not the Popular Vote, Will Decide Who Governs

We assess that before the vote only one institution can change the arithmetic—the Supreme Court, by 4 October. After it, the outcome runs through a small set of veto players—Liberman, the Haredi councils of Torah sages, Ra’am and the Joint List—and through President Herzog, whose discretion over the mandate and appetite for brokering a broad government are unusually wide. Likelihoods in the table are our analytic judgments.

Government-Formation Pathways: Every Route to Investiture on Mainstream Numbers Breaks a Stated Red Line

We judge that the recommendations to the President and the confidence vote, not the seat count on election night, will decide who governs. On Kan’s 27 September numbers and on the mainstream average, every path to a sworn-in government needs at least one actor to break a stated red line: Liberman (no minority government, no reliance on Arab or Haredi parties), Eisenkot (no Ra’am, no Haredi party without universal service, no Netanyahu, no rotation), Hendel–Zelekha (no narrow and no Arab-dependent government), Winter (a draft law before any government forms) and the Haredi parties (publicly uncommitted).11,40,13,14,55,280 The 2021 Bennett precedent shows that such lines can move after an election.32

The First Mandate: A Roughly Even Chance Between Netanyahu and Eisenkot

We assess that Netanyahu and Eisenkot have a roughly even chance of receiving the first mandate. In 2019–22 the President tasked the candidate with the most recommendations even without a path to a majority—Netanyahu in April 2021 “despite low chance of success”—and Herzog says his discretion is “broad” while refusing to say whether a minority government would be acceptable.15,358,340,16 Netanyahu gets the mandate if the Haredi parties recommend him or if Bennett and Liberman withhold support from Eisenkot; Eisenkot gets it only with a united change bloc plus either Haredi abstention or Ra’am’s recommendation. A first mandate does not mean a government.

Investiture Arithmetic: What Passes, and Whose Line It Breaks

We judge that every configuration that respects all stated positions fails: a narrow Netanyahu government loses 48–72 on Kan’s numbers (52–68 even with Amcha, whose condition it would breach), an Eisenkot government with the Joint List voting against loses 56–59, and the variant that honours Liberman’s pledge loses 48–52. Investiture requires more votes for than against, so abstentions decide close votes.22,39

We judge Yisrael Beytenu’s roughly nine seats to be the practical veto over an Eisenkot minority government: with Liberman and both Arab lists abstaining, the change side’s “for” column on Kan’s numbers falls to 47 against 52; Ra’am’s votes would lift it only to a 52–52 tie, which fails (48–52 if Hendel–Zelekha also abstains, E4). Ra’am, the only Arab list openly seeking inclusion, turns the 56–59 defeat into a 61–59 win.306,33 The Joint List’s support is conditional and its tone hardened after the bans; Balad leans against.302,303

Repeat election§11 (deemed dissolution) or a dissolution law setting a date up to five months out22,118Herzog: “I will do everything so that there will not be repeat elections”16—S5, 41% (about 53% if the Knesset is hung)

We assess that the public offers no mandate for any single exit. N12/Midgam (6 August) found 23% favouring a change government with Arab parties abstaining, 22% unity with a Netanyahu-led Likud, 21% unity with Likud without Netanyahu and 21% a repeat election; Zman Yisrael/Tatika (16–17 September) found 31.4% preferring a repeat election, 29.6% a unity government and 27% a minority government; and Channel 13 (23 September) found a broad unity government the plurality preference at 31%.363,364,4 Kan (18 August) found 37% saying Netanyahu should join a unity government he does not head, 36% against.365

The Rerun Clock: Deadlock Keeps Netanyahu in Office Into Mid-2027

We judge that a deadlock keeps Netanyahu in office as caretaker until a rerun four to seven months after the vote and, in most cases, for two to three more months while the next government forms (58–82 days in 2020–22)—which is why deadlock leaves him prime minister on 1 June 2027 in about 88% of cases. If every mandate fails, the Knesset is deemed dissolved and the election falls on the last Tuesday before day 90—between about early March and late May 2027, or about 9 June with a holiday postponement; the new Knesset could instead pass a dissolution law.22,118 Throughout, the outgoing government keeps full legal powers, subject to a duty of restraint that tightens after an election, and the prime minister may not dissolve the Knesset from its first sitting until a government is formed; the 2019–20 caretaker government served for more than a year and a half.22,24 From the 26th Knesset, incapacity can be declared only on physical or mental grounds.23 The non-Netanyahu majority would, however, control procedure at the first sitting on 10 November—the Speaker, the Arrangements Committee and the date in any dissolution law—as the Ra’am-backed bloc did in April 2021 and the incoming majority did in December 2022.361,366,367

Precedents, 2019–2022: The Most-Recommended Candidate Gets the First Mandate, Not Necessarily a Government

ElectionRecommendationsFirst mandateOutcome
April 2019Netanyahu 65, Gantz 45, none 1015Netanyahu (majority of recommenders)Failed; Knesset dissolved
September 2019Netanyahu 55, Gantz 54; Yisrael Beytenu and Balad none368Netanyahu, after failed unity talks; then GantzBoth failed; dissolution

Election Integrity and Wildcards: Fraud Has Almost No Chance; Contested Legitimacy and Late Shocks Are the Real Risks

We judge that fraud large enough to change the count has almost no chance (≤1%) and that postponement has almost no chance (about 1%; range 1–3%). The realistic risks are a slow or delegitimised result—which lengthens formation and favours the caretaker—and late security shocks that go unmeasured behind the poll blackout.

Postponement: Almost No Chance; Wartime Powers Are Local

  • Postponement. Extending the Knesset’s term needs a law passed by 80 MKs and “special circumstances” that prevent an election; no bill exists, and the only national precedent is 1973, when the election was moved by a vote of 67 to 1.59,65 We judge postponement remote (about 1%; range 1–3%), and conceivable only after a home-front war on the scale of February–March 2026.
  • Local powers. The CEC presidium may delay a station’s opening, suspend and resume voting, relocate stations and extend voting by up to 24 hours past the 22:00 close (Elections Law §70A).67 A 2026 temporary provision (§132A, passed 16 July) lets the CEC chair, after consulting the Chief of Staff, override Civil Defense orders at polling sites during a declared “special situation in the home front”—but not sirens or attack warnings.373,374,67 The government can declare such a situation subject to approval by the Knesset’s Foreign Affairs and Defence Committee; the Defence Minister declared one nationwide on 28 February 2026.375
  • Siren protocol. The presidium decided on 30 July that on a siren voting stops, a camera records, and a neutral official takes the sealed ballot box to shelter.346,376 Soldiers vote from 20 October (troops in Gaza get a full week) and evacuees at dedicated stations using double envelopes.107,67

List Disqualifications: A Mobilisation and Legitimacy Fight Whatever the Court Decides

We assess that the disqualification fight is itself a mobilisation and legitimacy issue, whatever the court decides. Kan (27 September) found 55% supporting the ban on the Joint List and 54% the ban on Ra’am, while pluralities of change-bloc voters opposed both (41% and 48%).1,31 Knesset Member Simcha Rothman, speaking for the coalition factions, called the 23 September decision “a very important achievement of the whole camp”; Ahmad Tibi said that “whoever fears competing with us at the ballot box tries to delete us from the ballot”.87,377

Foreign Influence and AI: Aimed More at Trust in the Result Than at a Side

  • Influence operations. Golden Owl, a private AI-based open-source firm, analysed 21,533 posts from 22 August to 22 September and flagged 1,516, identifying 237 suspicious accounts of which 57 belong to a foreign campaign. Its Iran-linked personas push 7 October blame, the draft and emigration, pre-emptive “stolen election” claims and false reports that the vote has been postponed or moved to 26 October. The Iran attribution is the firm’s own; no official Israeli attribution was found.53,378
  • Gaps in defence. Acting CEC director-general Dean Livne says foreign interest in this election is “second in the world, after the US midterms”, and the State Comptroller says the government has no policy against foreign meddling.54 The Shin Bet declined responsibility for countering domestic bot-driven campaigns, leaving them to police who lack the technology.348
  • Israel’s first generative-AI election. About 45% of AI campaign material comes from Netanyahu’s campaign (an Open University project reported by Bloomberg). CEC rules require labels—a watermark covering at least 5% of an image and spoken disclaimers on audio—but removal comes only after publication; a Netanyahu campaign AI ad showing Eisenkot embracing Mansour Abbas was taken down, and the CEC chair ordered a Yashar! AI video of soldiers removed on 6 August.379,380,381,382,383 We judge anonymous audio or video deepfakes on election day the most acute uncovered risk.
  • Counter-narratives. On 9 September Likud alleged “blatant foreign interference” by Palestinian politician Mohammed Dahlan behind Haaretz’s report of a UAE warning before 7 October and demanded criminal probes; it presented no evidence, and Haaretz stands by its reporting.48,49

Turnout and Mobilisation: Both Camps Are Organising; the Court and the CEC Leadership Have Checked the Main Frictions So Far

  • The High Court on 17 September struck down real-time relaying of who has voted (the “Elector” apps), after the coalition-majority CEC plenum had reinstated the practice 19–11; Levin called the ruling “an attempt to tilt the elections”, and Communications Minister Shlomo Karhi had vowed in August to “obtain the information any way available to us”.47,384,385,386,387
  • i24NEWS reports that Likud will revive its “Arabs are flocking to the polls” message to mobilise its own voters, using the CEC’s first-ever publication of turnout by polling station; the opposition parties have set up joint turnout teams.388,315
  • The CEC chair issued an interim order on 25 September barring a Satmar-linked fund from paying people, reportedly $200, not to vote.329,389 Likud’s lawyer has called drives to fly expatriates home to vote “bribery”.390,54
  • Calls by Minister David Amsalem and MK Amit Halevi for cameras in every polling station were rejected; Livne replied that “there are no forgeries in Israel; if any, marginal”.391,392

Pressure on the CEC: Sustained, and Read by Some Legal Scholars as Groundwork for a Dispute

We assess that the CEC enters the vote under sustained pressure from Likud and coalition figures, which several legal scholars quoted by the Times of Israel read as groundwork for disputing a bad result; Likud denies this (not independently verified).46 Long-serving director-general Orly Adas resigned on 30 April after a Likud legal challenge to her term; Likud figures attacked her successor, Livne, as politically biased; and the Knesset Finance Committee cut the CEC’s budget request by NIS 50m in February.393,394,395,396,397 Haredi anti-draft protesters rioted outside Justice Sohlberg’s home in June.398 Livne has warned that “one of the players is trying to cast doubt on the results even before there are results”.395

Contested Result: Likely, and It Would Lengthen Formation

We judge a public dispute over the result’s legitimacy by at least one camp likely (60–70%), peaking if a bloc lands at 60–62 or a list sits at the threshold during the central count of roughly 500,000 double-envelope ballots about two days after polling; appeals run to 18 November.54,60 The risk runs both ways: Arab and opposition parties would contest a narrow Netanyahu win if the bans stand, and coalition parties would contest a change government formed with Arab votes. Asked how they would feel if their side lost, 27% of Israelis said they would respect whatever the public decides; a loss would be “intolerable” for 81% on the left, 62% in the centre-left and 49% in the centre, against 20% on the right (JPPI, May).51 Fear of a compromised vote is highest on the left and in the centre.152,324 A single Times of Israel report of 17 July, which we could not independently verify, quotes opposition leader Yair Lapid as saying, “I don’t think the Netanyahu government will try to interfere in the elections. I know it,” and IDI’s Assaf Shapira as saying that Likud representatives abstained on certifying the 2020 result.46 The practical effect would be to lengthen formation, which favours the caretaker (S5).

Wildcards Before 27 October: Late Shocks Mostly Move Seats Within the Right

EventProbability before 27 OctoberLikely effect

Low-Probability, High-Impact Events

  • An upheld ban on both Arab lists (about 5–7%) would remove the Ra’am route, collapse Arab representation and turnout, and—on mainstream numbers—bring a Zionist-only change majority within reach while triggering an emergency meeting of Arab leaders on a boycott or counter-mobilisation. A Joint List-only ban (≈10%) would not remove the Ra’am route.377,43
  • A kidnapping followed by the razing of Gaza City that Katz has threatened, or a mass-casualty barrage from Iran or Hezbollah during the final week, is the only pre-vote shock with a large effect of uncertain direction; the poll blackout from the end of Friday 23 October means it would go unmeasured.169,114
  • A Netanyahu health event through mid-2027 (remote, ≈3%) is the fastest route to a Likud government without him (S6); he is 76 and disclosed in April 2026 that he had been treated for early-stage prostate cancer and declared cancer-free; no new health incident has been reported.403
  • A viral election-day deepfake of a leader, or a threshold list flipped by the double-envelope count, would fuel a legitimacy crisis rather than change bloc arithmetic.379,54
  • After the vote. A US–Iran round after 3 November would coincide with official results (4 November), the new Knesset’s first sitting (10 November) and the President’s consultations; it would strengthen the caretaker, revive emergency-unity formulas (S2) and lengthen any deadlock (S5).116,117

Alternative Analysis: Structured Challenges Leave the Call Intact but Widen Its Error Bands

We judge that three structured challenges—a key assumptions check, an analysis of competing hypotheses and a red-team review—leave the direction of the call intact but show that the estimates are more precise than the evidence allows. The three estimator lenses are one judgment presented three times, and the weakest assumption that carries real weight is that pre-election red lines will hold.

Analysis of Competing Hypotheses

We tested three hypotheses: H1, Netanyahu forms a government (S1+S2); H2, the opposition forms one (S3+S4); and H3, deadlock, a repeat election or another outcome (S5+S6). Before scoring, S6 was cleaned up: a unity government with Netanyahu serving first belongs in H1, a Likud government without Netanyahu is a non-Netanyahu government, and a postponement keeps Netanyahu in office.

#EvidenceCredibilityH1H2H3
E1Six mainstream firms: Netanyahu bloc 51 with Amcha (47 without); no mainstream poll above 54 in September, and only one (Channel 13, 15–16 September) above 531,30,45HighI+NC
E2Mainstream polls under-read the Netanyahu bloc in four of five elections (3.4 seats in 2022) and Shas in five of five; Channel 14 shows 63, i24NEWS 60, DRI 59; Direct Polls was closest in 202217,8MediumCIN
E3Estimates: hung Knesset 74–79%; Netanyahu bloc ≥61 8–15%; change bloc plus Ra’am ≥61: 25–30% without Hendel–Zelekha, 42–47% with it7MediumICC
E4Liberman: no Netanyahu; no minority or Arab-dependent government (“58 seats … we won’t be there”); Yisrael Beytenu backed banning Ra’am and the Joint List11,36HighI+IC
E5Eisenkot: never with Netanyahu; rotation “not on the table”; Ra’am not in his government “at the moment”—but Arab parties may “support, abstain or be absent”13,12,288HighICN
E6Haredim: Shas “we support Netanyahu, period”, but its Council of Torah Sages would decide on following him into a rerun; Degel “no trust”; Agudah “not committed”; reported plan to recommend no one278,279,282,343,280MediumIIC

Weighted inconsistency (I = 1, I+ = 2, halved when credibility is medium)8.03.01.0

Key: C consistent, N neutral, I inconsistent, I+ strongly inconsistent. Unweighted counts of inconsistent items: H1 7 (two strong), H2 5, H3 2. E1 is rated high because it is a direct, current measurement on which six firms agree; its known weaknesses (herding and past under-reading of the right) are carried separately in E2 and in Key Assumption 3 rather than counted twice.

We read the matrix cautiously. H3 has the least inconsistent evidence and H1 the most, but H3 wins partly because it is the default: every stated veto is consistent with nobody forming anything, and about half its lead comes from campaign statements (E4–E6, E9) that the same actors reversed in 2020–21. Downgrading all of those to medium credibility drops H2 to 2.5; H3 still leads. Liberman (E4) is the most diagnostic single item—the only one inconsistent with both H1 and H2. If the right-leaning pollsters are right, E1 flips and H1 falls to about 6, still trailing, because Liberman blocks Netanyahu at any seat count. The analysis ranks consistency, not probability, and H3 postpones rather than answers the question: S5 is 41%, H3 47%.

The Red Team’s Main Challenges

  • Overprecision. Headline S5 figures of 45, 42 and 38 hid sensitivity runs spanning 38–50 for S5 and 13–26 for S4; conversion rates such as 0.47, 0.13 and 0.07 are judgments fitted to four linked hung Knessets involving the same people. We therefore report ranges.
  • Anchoring on the polls (all lenses put the Netanyahu bloc at 51–53 and let a 10–20% weight on the right-leaning polls (12% in the quantitative model) carry S1; none modelled a late return of soft Likud voters from a first-time party that has already slipped from its peak), on the unadjusted base rate, and on red lines treated as near-hard constraints despite 2020–21.
  • Missing mechanisms. (a) A face-saving route for Ra’am: Eisenkot’s fourth principle (“Hamas is a terror group to be destroyed”) and Yisrael Beytenu’s test are criteria, not vetoes on who Ra’am is (S4 +1–2). (b) Rotation can be re-legislated by a 61-seat coalition. (c) Joint lists can split: Hadash–Ta’al abstaining while Balad votes against turns some losses into narrow wins (S4 +1). (d) Emergency unity in wartime, the only plausible way to bend “never with Netanyahu” (S2 +1). (e) The non-Netanyahu majority controls procedure at the first sitting and the date in any dissolution law (−1 to −3 on the 1 June figure). (f) Late consolidation or a new-party fade (a fatter S1 tail). (g) Certification friction over the double-envelope count (favours S5).
  • Framing. H3 postpones rather than answers “who forms the next government”. Counting a 2027 rerun as roughly a coin flip, Netanyahu heads the next government actually sworn in with about 35–40% (on the same basis, Eisenkot about 40–45%); he is the likeliest person to hold the office on any given date through mid-2027, while Eisenkot is the likeliest head of any government newly formed in this Knesset.
  • Adopted changes. S4 +2 (conversion about 0.5 where the opposition plus Ra’am cleanly reaches 61); S2 +1; S3 −1; S1 set between the Quant and Contrarian figures; RZ–Zehut’s pass rate cut to about 90%; Ra’am and Joint List ban risks separated.
  • Fairness check. The red team found no systematic partisan tilt in the fact-checks of the requester’s chat: corrections cut both ways. The problem was inconsistency between research slices, resolved in Annex A.

Alternative View: The Red Lines Bend and Eisenkot Forms a Government

The strongest dissent from our call comes from the coalition lens’s high-flexibility case, and it is echoed by our public-sentiment analyst, who put an anti-Netanyahu government at 45–60%, and by prediction markets, which price Eisenkot at 52.9%.28 On this view we overweight deadlock because we treat campaign statements as near-hard constraints:

  • The same actors bent before. In 2020 Liberman recommended Gantz alongside the Joint List, and Gantz then joined Netanyahu; in 2021 Liberman sat as finance minister in a government that included Ra’am, sworn in 60–59 with three Ra’am votes for and one Ra’am abstention.38,369,32,39
  • The 2026 lines are criteria, not vetoes. Eisenkot hedges with “at the moment”; Yisrael Beytenu’s test is “eliminating Hamas”; Mansour Abbas has said Israel “was born a Jewish state and will remain so”; and Segalovitz calls Hamas a terror organisation that must be destroyed. One post-election statement by Abbas would be the cheapest climb-down for Liberman and Eisenkot.12,37,220
  • Their voters would tolerate it. About two-thirds of Jewish opposition voters would back a government relying on Ra’am in a tie, and model estimates of the opposition plus Ra’am reaching 61 range from about 25–30% without Hendel–Zelekha, whose leaders reject reliance on Arab votes, to 42–47% with it.198,199,7
  • The system pushes against a rerun. Herzog has pledged to do “everything” to avoid one, and a wartime rerun is costly.16

If this view is right, S4 rises to about 30% and S5 falls to about 30%, pulling the probability that Netanyahu is prime minister on 1 June 2027 to about 46%—a roughly even chance, which would reverse our “likely” call. Why we do not adopt it: the escape valves of 2021 are gone—statutory rotation is repealed, voters are not exhausted by a string of elections as they were in 2021, and Liberman voted on 23 September to ban both Arab lists; the Joint List hardened after the bans, calling the opposition “not a real alternative”; and in about a third of hung draws a change government also needs Joint List abstention, which it withheld in 2021, and in about a quarter it has no Arab-supported path at all.20,36,303 We hold S4 at 21% with a plausible range up to 30%. A second, opposite dissent—the contrarian lens’s—holds that election-night tails are fatter on both sides, with a Netanyahu-bloc majority near 15% and Likud finishing as the largest party near 45%, on the pattern of late consolidation (2015) and new-party fades (2021).

Indicators and Signposts: Watch the Court, the Final Raw Filings and Liberman’s Wording

We recommend re-running the estimate three times before the vote: after the Supreme Court rulings (by 4 October), after the surplus-vote and list-publication deadlines (16–19 October), and after the last permitted polls (Friday 23 October).
