Implications: Continuity on Security Under Any Outcome; Rule of Law and Relations With Europe Would Move First

Gaza and the West Bank. We judge a near-term move toward Palestinian statehood or an IDF withdrawal from the Yellow Line very unlikely under any outcome. Netanyahu’s stated end state is a demilitarised Gaza under overriding Israeli security control and no Palestinian state “as long as I am prime minister”; Eisenkot opposes a Palestinian state; and Bennett, according to Arab48, would not withdraw from the Yellow Line before Hamas is fully disarmed.27,1,196 Israel’s formal commitment remains the US-led plan, whose text says Israel will not occupy or annex Gaza; the plan has largely stalled and is parked until after the vote.166,164 In the modal caretaker outcome current policy persists into 2027—E1 tenders, accelerated outpost legalisation and the Religious Zionism–Otzma Yehudit agenda of annexation to the Yellow Line and “voluntary emigration” retain influence.181,183,275,186 We judge that an opposition-led government would change rule-of-law policy, enforcement against settler violence and 7 October accountability faster than the military posture in Gaza, Lebanon or toward Iran.

US–Israel. We assess Washington to be the one outside actor with plausible direct leverage on the vote and, more so, on formation. Trump has withheld an endorsement while saying he “may endorse somebody”, and his reported plan to resume bombing Iran after 3 November would fall inside the formation window, strengthening the caretaker and emergency-unity formulas.351,116 Israeli trust in him has fallen—10% have “great trust” that he will do right by the relationship, down from 34% six months earlier—although US ties matter to the vote of half the electorate.154,152 In the modal outcome a caretaker Netanyahu remains Washington’s interlocutor on Iran and Gaza into 2027.

Europe. We judge relations with Western Europe will stay strained under any outcome in the near term, but a change government would probably lower the temperature faster. The UK, France and Canada have committed to national bans on settlement goods, the UK has tightened arms licensing, and Israel retaliated against the UK; the EU-27 remain divided, and the ICC warrant for Netanyahu stands.252,182,254,257,265 A senior UK official called the argument that the measures help Netanyahu and Smotrich “a problematic point”, and Lapid, at least, has reportedly opposed sanctions, so a change of government would ease tone before it changes substance.254,251

The region. We assess that regional actors are hedging rather than betting on the outcome. The UAE channel remains active (Mohammed bin Zayed received Netanyahu on 27 September), Saudi normalisation is off the table before the vote, Lebanon talks are due to resume in October with the IDF holding a security zone, and the Houthis’ reported seizure of the Bab al-Mandab coast in September has opened a Saudi–Houthi front.206,352,179,177,406,407,408 A long caretaker period that coincides with a renewed US–Iran round would leave Israel’s regional posture unchanged into 2027.

Intelligence Gaps and Collection Priorities: Three Gaps Drive Most of the Uncertainty

We assess that three gaps account for most of the uncertainty in this assessment; each has a dated collection priority.

  1. Where Netanyahu’s bloc really stands—about 51 seats or about 60. This decides the size of the S1 zone relative to the deadlock zone. No house discloses its weighting targets (2022 recalled vote, religiosity, turnout screen) or how it allocates undecided voters; the mainstream houses herd; and Next Data and DRI have no election record.131,144,143 Collection: parse every CEC raw filing through 23 October rather than published seat counts; track raw Likud and undecided shares in Channel 14 and i24NEWS; after the vote, compare exit polls with the count.
  2. How far the pivotal red lines bend after the vote—Liberman above all, then the Joint List, the Haredi rabbis and Eisenkot. On mainstream numbers every route to a sworn-in government breaks at least one stated line; this is the S4-versus-S5 hinge (±10 points). No written directive from any Council of Torah Sages exists; Deri’s reported remark that the sages will decide whether to follow Netanyahu into a rerun rests on a single report; Ra’am’s price for outside support is not public; no post-ban Joint List statement on the investiture vote was found; and Herzog declined to say whether a minority government would be acceptable.279,340 Collection: the Haredi press (Yated Ne’eman and Hamodia were not read directly), Arabic-language media (Panet was read only once and Kul al-Arab not at all), final-fortnight interviews on N12 and Kan, and Knesset procedural moves on 10 November.
  3. The Arab variable—ballot access, turnout and Ra’am’s threshold. This decides whether S4 exists and whether S3 becomes reachable. No case number or hearing date for Ra’am’s appeal had been found by 28 September; the only post-ban turnout reading comes via posts on X; no survey after the court ruling exists yet; and the Negev picture rests on aggregates.332,409 Collection: the rulings of 1–4 October and whether and when Ra’am’s appeal is heard; Stat-Net, Afkar and Abraham Initiatives surveys; statements by the Follow-Up Committee and the Islamic Movement; CEC per-station turnout on election day. Every model should be re-run on 5 October.

Secondary Gaps

  • Access limits. Maariv blocked automated retrieval, so its poll figures come from search excerpts and secondary reports; Haaretz and some Globes items were read at headline or lede level only; the Channel 16 and Israel Hayom figures rest partly on the Wikipedia index; a Kan figure of 45% of Arab voters “sure to vote” circulates but is not in Kan’s published article.
  • Unresolved figures. Whether Israel barred 11 or 12 UK officials; the cumulative 38 UK sanctions designations; Gaza deaths since the ceasefire (1,394 according to UNRWA and the Health Ministry, 1,145 according to AP); the Stat-Net post-ban figures; Lapid’s reported opposition to sanctions and Dahlia Scheindlin’s “no real backlash” (both from one Al Jazeera report); Lapid’s reported remark on government interference and whether Likud representatives abstained on certifying the 2020 result (both from one Times of Israel report).
  • Legal and procedural. The filing status of surplus agreements (knowable only on 19 October); how the CEC would treat a list that withdraws after 18 October; the current legal status of the July 2026 law curbing the Attorney General’s role; Knesset rules on fast-tracking bills before a government forms; the official gazette citation of the §13A repeal.
  • Segments without 2026 data. Russian-speaking voters, Druze voters, Haredi turnout, and age breakdowns beyond first-time voters.
  • Official assessments. No public Shin Bet or National Cyber Directorate assessment of foreign interference; Golden Owl’s Iran attribution is unconfirmed.
  • Health. No independent information beyond Netanyahu’s April 2026 disclosure that he was treated for early-stage prostate cancer and declared cancer-free.
  • The Smotrich clip. The caption’s closing sentence rests on the machine transcript of Channel 14’s YouTube upload; the broadcast was not reviewed in full.
  • Cutoff. Information to 28 September 2026; reference texts (laws, timetables and calendars) were re-accessed on 29 September to confirm them, and no later information was used. The Supreme Court rulings of 1–4 October postdate this assessment.

Annex A: Claims Circulating in the Requester’s Chat

We assess the chat’s factual claims as broadly accurate and its framing of the election as a direct verdict on Netanyahu as misleading. The verdicts below reconcile the research slices, several of which rated the Smotrich clip “unverified” before Channel 14’s own text was located.

ClaimAssessmentEvidence
Telegram clip (“ResistanceTrench”; Channel 14 logo): Finance Minister Smotrich on Gaza—“The biggest key to migration is: take away their hope. If you annex up to the Yellow Line … they will start searching for solutions for where to go. That is the right thing.”True (authentic, shortened)Channel 14’s own write-up of its Sukkot interview prints the core passage; the closing clause appears in the machine transcript of Channel 14’s YouTube upload of the same broadcast; Anadolu’s Arabic service and Palestine International Broadcast corroborate. The caption omits harsher lines, so it understates rather than overstates what Smotrich said. See the box below.241,410,411,412
“A new centrist party has been established, which currently leads in several polls (with Likud coming second)”Mostly trueThe party is Eisenkot’s Yashar!, founded on 16 September 2025 and running for the first time.413,414 It leads the latest poll of six of nine firms, with Likud second in each: Kan 23–21 (27 September), N12 23–20 (22 September), Channel 13 22–18 (23 September), Maariv 22–18 (23–24 September), Channel 16 23–19 (24 September) and Zman Yisrael 23–21 (23–24 September). Likud leads in Channel 14 (32–22), i24NEWS (27–22) and DRI (26–18), and an average that includes those houses puts Likud narrowly ahead (Maariv’s poll of polls, 27 September: 23.0 to 22.2).1,30,4,45,6,5,8,121,10,123 The lead is narrowing. “Centrist” is contested: Eisenkot calls himself “a man of the centre with a security approach”, The Economist calls the party centre-right, and he opposes a Palestinian state.225,415 Leading as a party is not leading as a bloc: his four-list camp polls 52–55 in mainstream polls, short of 61.
“Two other former PMs, Bennett and Lapid (centre-right & centre-left), have joined forces to beat Netanyahu”TrueThey run as one list, Together, led by Bennett (ביחד), announced on 26 April 2026, with Bennett first and Lapid second.308,416,417 It is a joint list of two registered parties that can split after the vote, and it has fallen from about 25–26 seats in April–May to 11–13.75,130 The labels are loose: Bennett describes himself as a right-wing liberal Zionist, and Lapid is usually described as centrist. On 26 September both signed a five-leader non-aggression pact with Eisenkot, Liberman and Golan that names no candidate for prime minister.315
“In four weeks a lot can happen still” (the election is about four weeks away)TrueElection day is Tuesday 27 October 2026, 29 days after 28 September.60 Pending before then: the Supreme Court rulings on the Arab lists (by 4 October), surplus-vote agreements (16 October), publication of the lists (18 October) and the last permitted polls (23 October). Historically, bloc movement in the final month is small; the live uncertainty lies in threshold outcomes, turnout, the court ruling and security shocks.17
Most of the world, including France and the UK, has changed its position on Israel, as last week’s UN speeches showedPartly true (overstated)France and the UK: true—both recognised Palestine in September 2025; on 8 September 2026 the UK announced a ban on settlement goods (legislation within six to nine months) and tighter arms licensing, France and Canada pledged national bans, and nine other states signed a statement of intent.418,252,182 UN week: true—dozens of delegates walked out as Netanyahu began speaking on 24 September, and several leaders criticised Israel from the podium (see Political Climate).262,260 “Most of the world” is overstated at government level: the US declined to follow, Germany, Italy, Austria, Czechia and Hungary resist EU-wide measures, and Gulf engagement continues.254,257,206 It does match global public opinion (Pew, 36 countries: median 67% unfavourable).268 The governmental shift mostly predates “last week”, and it concerns settlements and conduct in Gaza, not Israel’s legitimacy: Macron and Burnham also condemned Hamas and 7 October.
Re-electing Netanyahu “after everything” would reveal a lot about IsraelisOpinion; premise misleadingIsraelis vote for party lists, not for a prime minister. The single most likely path (41%) keeps Netanyahu in office as caretaker without any re-election, and a Likud plurality would not guarantee that he forms a government.22 His bloc polls 49–53 in the six mainstream polls and 59–63 in the three right-leaning ones; only Channel 14 (63) shows a majority. 71% say his UN speech will not affect their vote.1
“That will be the test after which all masks are off … Or hope returns (which I doubt).”OpinionNot checkable. An ambiguous outcome—deadlock, a rerun or unity—is more likely than a clear verdict either way; see Implications for what each outcome would change.

The Smotrich Quote: Source, Original and What the English Rendering Shortened

Programme and date. Channel 14’s Sukkot special «בסוכה של ריקלין» (“In Riklin’s Sukkah”), hosted by Shimon Riklin, with Finance Minister Bezalel Smotrich; aired on the evening of Sunday 27 September 2026 (Channel 14’s web write-up is timestamped 23:24 Israel time) and rerun on 28 September at 05:30 and 11:30. Anadolu’s Arabic service, dating the interview to “Monday”, apparently reported the rerun.241,410,411

Hebrew original (Channel 14’s text, section “The Gaza campaign: take away their hope”): «המפתח הכי גדול להגירה, זה קח להם את התקווה. כל עוד הם חושבים שיום יבוא ויהיה להם שם שיקום… ואם אתה מספח עד הקו הצהוב וזה שלנו, ואז [עזה] נשארת קטנטנה וצפופה והרוסה, כולם יבינו שאין מה לחפש שם. אנחנו לא יכולים להרשות לעצמנו שני מיליון נאצים חיים על הגדרות שלנו. ולכן צריך לספח עד הקו הצהוב, ולהגיד, חברים, זה מחיר הטעות שלכם.» Channel 14 printed «אני נשארת» where the bracket stands, apparently a transcription slip. The caption’s closing clause appears in the machine transcript of the YouTube upload: «יתחילו לחפש פתרונות לאן ללכת, זה הדבר הנכון».241,410

Our translation. “The biggest key to emigration is: take away their hope. As long as they think a day will come when there will be reconstruction there … if you annex up to the Yellow Line and it is ours, and [Gaza] stays tiny, crowded and destroyed, everyone will understand there is nothing to look for there. We cannot allow ourselves two million Nazis living on our fences. So we must annex up to the Yellow Line and say: friends, this is the price of your mistake.”

Context. In the same answer Smotrich called for annexation “up to the Litani and certainly up to the Yellow Line” as the way to “voluntary emigration”, conceding that Egypt keeps its border closed and that no country will take Gazans. On Ynet’s “120 ve-Achat” podcast, released the same weekend (26–27 September), he urged annexing “at least up to the Yellow Line” and going “to war in Judea and Samaria, to do there what we did in Gaza” to dismantle the Palestinian Authority. The Yellow Line area covers roughly 58–64% of Gaza.410,76,186,419,420 These are campaign statements, not government policy, by the finance minister, whose list polls four to seven seats and whose agenda would keep its influence in a caretaker period (see Implications); Israel’s formal commitment remains the US-led plan, which ties withdrawal to Hamas’s disarmament. Within about 24 hours no reaction to this clip from an Israeli party or a Western government was found; reactions (the Palestinian foreign ministry, Adalah, Turkish outlets) concerned mainly the Ynet remarks.419,421

What the English rendering shortened. The caption is a faithful but compressed translation. It drops the conditional that frames the passage (“as long as they think a day will come when there will be reconstruction there”), the sentence “We cannot allow ourselves two million Nazis living on our fences” and the closing “this is the price of your mistake”; it renders הגירה, which in context means emigration, as “migration”; and its final sentence rests on the machine transcript rather than Channel 14’s written excerpt.

What the result will and will not reveal. The seat count will not be a clean referendum on Netanyahu: in 2022 his bloc out-polled its rivals by about 30,000 votes—under one seat—yet won 64 seats because 8.76% of votes were wasted, and in 2026 a Likud plurality, a hung Knesset or a long caretaker period could each coexist with the 61% who told pollsters in June that he should not run again.96,95,321 What it can show is how voters weigh two stable features of opinion against each other: a hawkish policy consensus (72% of Jews would not agree “under any condition” to withdraw from the Gaza security perimeter; 17% of Israelis think a Palestinian state could coexist peacefully with Israel) and broad dissatisfaction with the government’s performance and record on 7 October (75% back a state commission of inquiry; 27% have high trust in the government), alongside Netanyahu’s standing as the single most preferred candidate for prime minister (36%).193,325,191 Because any national result averages over sharply different communities, it will say more about turnout and coalition bargaining than about “Israelis” as a single body.

Annex B: Estimative Language—What We Mean When We Say

We use the estimative terms of Intelligence Community Directive 203, tied to the probability ranges below, and nothing else to express likelihood. The first use of a term for a key number gives the number in parentheses. Terms describe likelihood; confidence levels describe the quality of the evidence and reasoning behind a judgment, and the two are reported separately. A probability can be low with high confidence, or the single most likely outcome can still be “unlikely”: at 41%, deadlock and a 2027 rerun (S5) is unlikely on this scale, yet it is the most likely of the six scenarios.

TermProbability rangeExample in this assessment
Almost no chance / remote01–05%Fraud large enough to change the count (≤1%); postponement by 80 MKs (1–3%)
Very unlikely / highly improbable05–20%Netanyahu’s bloc reaches 61 and forms (S1, 10%); Netanyahu forms any new government in this Knesset (19%)
Unlikely / improbable20–45%A change government relying on Ra’am (S4, 21%); deadlock and a 2027 rerun (S5, 41%)
Roughly even chance / roughly even odds45–55%No new government, or another outcome (H3, 47%)
Likely / probable55–80%A hung Knesset (≈75–80%); Netanyahu still prime minister on 1 June 2027 (56%)
Very likely / highly probable80–95%The Supreme Court reinstates Ra’am (≈93%) and the Joint List (≈85%)
Almost certain / nearly certain95–99%Voting takes place on 27 October as scheduled (≥97%)

Confidence in Our Judgments

  • High confidence generally indicates that a judgment rests on high-quality information from multiple independent sources, or that the nature of the issue makes a solid judgment possible. It is not a certainty: such judgments can still be wrong.
  • Moderate confidence means that the information is credibly sourced and plausible but not of sufficient quality or corroboration to warrant a higher level—for example, where polls agree but share a common method, or where estimates share one evidence base.
  • Low confidence means that the information is scant, questionable or fragmented, that it is difficult to make solid analytic inferences, or that the judgment rests on assumptions—such as whether politicians keep campaign red lines—that cannot be tested before the event.

Annex C: Methodology and Sourcing

Team process. The assessment was produced in six stages. (1) Ten research analysts collected in Hebrew, Arabic and English on ten slices: rules and calendar; polls; the Netanyahu camp; the centre and opposition; the Haredi parties; the Arab lists; public sentiment; security and foreign affairs; the economy and the campaign; and institutions and election integrity. (2) Ten independent verifiers re-checked every load-bearing claim against primary or independent sources and recorded corrections; where a verifier corrected or refuted a finding, the verified version governs this assessment. (3) A completeness review commissioned six gap-fill tasks: the Arab lists and a conditional seat model of the Arab vote; pollster accuracy, house effects and a canonical bias-aware baseline; a recommendation and investiture matrix; threshold arithmetic from raw CEC filings; ways out of a deadlock; and a dated event calendar with the law on voting under fire. (4) Three estimators—quantitative, coalition-bargaining and contrarian—worked separately, from the same verified dossier, to produce scenario probabilities. (5) A red team ran a key assumptions check, an analysis of competing hypotheses and a challenge to the probabilities, and recommended the final figures. (6) A four-lens review checked the draft before finalisation.

Sources. The team consulted 884 open sources—466 in Hebrew, 397 in English and 21 in Arabic by the language tag in our source register, which files about 25 Hebrew-language pages and one Arabic page under English, so Hebrew and Arabic sourcing is slightly understated—all listed and linked in the Complete Source List; those cited in the text appear in the Endnotes. Primary sources were preferred: CEC timetables, plenary protocols and poll filings; court orders; Knesset releases and the consolidated texts of the Basic Laws and the Elections Law; survey institutes’ own releases; and broadcasters’ own poll pages. Hebrew outlets of opposing leanings (for example Channel 14 and Haaretz, Israel Hayom and Ynet) were cross-checked on contested points. Haredi news sites (Kikar HaShabbat, Behadrei Haredim, JDN) and Arabic-language outlets (Arab48, Al-Araby al-Jadeed, Al-Mamlaka) were read in the original; the Haredi party dailies Yated Ne’eman and Hamodia were not read directly; Panet was read once and Kul al-Arab not at all (see Intelligence Gaps). Wikipedia served only as an index whose figures were checked against primary releases; where they differed, the primary release was used (Channel 13’s Shas and UTJ figures, for example, follow 13tv, not the index). Paywalled or blocked items (some Haaretz, Maariv and Globes pieces) were used only at the level actually read and are attributed; unverified items are labelled in the text.

Poll aggregation and seat modelling. The canonical baseline counts one poll per firm (Kantar and Lazar once each) and shows the three right-leaning houses separately. The bias-aware simulation runs 40,000 draws on raw CEC filings with corrections for the historical under-polling of Shas and Likud; the quantitative lens runs 100,000 draws with measured house effects, 2019–22 final-poll errors, threshold fade for new lists, Arab turnout and the risk of an Arab-list ban, and allocates seats by D’Hondt/Bader–Ofer with the reported surplus pairs. A formation layer, calibrated to the 2019–22 hung parliaments, converts seat outcomes into scenarios. Final scenario probabilities are the red team’s recommendations and should be read with their plausible ranges.

Limits. The three estimates share one dossier, one baseline and one base rate, so their agreement is not independent confirmation. No new research was run after 28 September 2026; reference texts (laws, timetables and calendars) were re-accessed on 29 September to confirm them, and no later information was used. The Supreme Court rulings due 1–4 October postdate this assessment.

Changes from the v0.9 draft memo (28 September 2026). No scenario probability changed. Two estimative labels were aligned to the ICD 203 bands—19% is now “very unlikely” (was “unlikely”) and 56% “likely” (was “roughly even, leaning likely”)—and the 14 draft judgments were consolidated into eight Key Judgments. The four-lens review of 29 September kept the reinstatement estimates for Ra’am (≈93%) and the Joint List (≈85%) separate throughout, which puts both lists together at about 80–85% (the draft said 85–90%); stated postponement as about 1% (range 1–3%; the draft said ≤2%); added a like-for-like figure for Eisenkot counting a 2027 rerun (about 40–45%); and corrected several figures, attributions and dates.

Annex D: Tasking

The optimised research prompt that tasked this assessment, reproduced verbatim:

ROLE
You are a team of senior political analysts (Hebrew-, Arabic- and English-reading) working to US intelligence-community analytic standards (ICD 203). You work from open sources only, speak for no agency, and are strictly non-partisan. You report to the Chief Political Analyst.

QUESTION
Who is most likely to form Israel's next government after the Knesset election due by 27 October 2026 — and how confident can we be?

DELIVERABLE
An Intelligence Assessment for the Chief Political Analyst, in HTML, in the format and language of US intelligence-community finished intelligence: judgment-led title, scope note, key judgments with calibrated likelihood and confidence, a clear position (the most likely outcome, a probability for each scenario, and the signposts that would change the call), and every source listed and clickable in an annex. "Position" means an analytic judgment of what will happen, not a preference for who should win.

COVER
1. Rules and calendar: election date, dissolution status, list deadlines, 3.25% threshold, surplus deals, 61-seat math, the President's mandate process.
2. Polls: every seat poll of the last 60 days (Hebrew and English outlets), averages by party and bloc, 6-month trend, pollster house effects, historical poll error.
3. Parties and blocs: each list near or above the threshold: leader, platform, trend, mergers, splits, declared coalition red lines.
4. Climate: Gaza ceasefire and the Yellow Line, hostages, Lebanon, Iran, West Bank annexation, 7 October accountability and the state commission of inquiry, Haredi draft, judicial overhaul, Netanyahu's trial and pardon request, economy and cost of living.
5. Citizen sentiment: trust, top issues, turnout intentions, by segment: secular, traditional, religious-Zionist, Haredi, Arab, Druze, Russian-speaking, reservists, young voters.
6. Stakeholders: party leaders; President, Attorney General, courts, Central Elections Committee, IDF and Shin Bet leadership; protest movements, hostage families, settler leadership, Haredi rabbis; the Trump administration, Gulf states, Europe; UN General Assembly week of September 2026.
7. Election-integrity risks: postponement attempts, list disqualifications, foreign influence operations, disinformation, turnout suppression.
8. Scenarios: 5-6 government-formation outcomes with probabilities, path to 61 seats, triggers, and the chance of a repeat election.
9. Signposts: what to watch in the final four weeks and on election night.
10. Fact-check: the claims in the attached chat (new centrist party leading polls with Likud second; Bennett and Lapid joining forces; election in about four weeks; France/UK shift; the Smotrich quote on Gaza).

METHOD
- Newest sources first (last 90 days); read Hebrew primary outlets (N12, Kan, Channel 13, Maariv, Ynet, Israel Hayom, Channel 14, Haaretz, Haredi and Arabic-language media), not just English summaries.
- Cross-check across outlets of opposing leanings. Date and source every data point.
- Separate facts from judgments. Use the ICD 203 estimative terms tied to % ranges and give a confidence level (high / moderate / low) for each key judgment.
- Verify every load-bearing claim with an independent source, close gaps found by a completeness review, and run a key-assumptions check, an analysis of competing hypotheses and a red-team review before finalising.

OUTPUT
Intelligence Assessment (HTML): cover with judgment-led title -> scope note -> key judgments -> scenario outlook -> discussion (mechanics, seat arithmetic, climate, parties, sentiment, stakeholders, formation pathways, integrity and wildcards) -> alternative analysis -> indicators and signposts -> implications -> intelligence gaps -> annexes (chat claims, estimative language, methodology, tasking) -> endnotes -> complete clickable source list -> change log.
